A Combination Theorem for Trees of Metric Bundles Thanks: This paper is part of R. Halder’s PhD thesis written under the supervision of P. Sardar.
Rakesh Halder

Abstract
. Motivated by the work of Bestvina-Feighn ( [ BF92 ] ) and Mj-Sardar ( [ MS12 ] ), we define trees of metric bundles subsuming both the trees of metric spaces and the metric bundles. Then we prove a combination theorem for these spaces. More precisely, we prove that a tree of metric bundles is hyperbolic if the following hold (see Theorem 1.4 ). ( 1 ) (1) The fibers are uniformly hyperbolic metric spaces and the base is also hyperbolic metric space, ( 2 ) (2) barycenter maps for the fibers are uniformly coarsely surjective, ( 3 ) (3) the edge spaces are uniformly qi embedded in the corresponding fibers and ( 4 ) (4) Bestvina-Feighn’s flaring condition is satisfied. As an application, we provide a combination theorem for certain complexes of groups over finite simplicial complex (see Theorem 1.2 ).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
20F65

1.
Introduction

Bestvina-Feighn (
[
BF92
]
) proved that the fundamental group of a finite graph of hyperbolic groups with the qi embedded condition and annuli flare condition is hyperbolic (see
[
BF96
, Theorem
1.2
]
). Motivated by this work of Bestvina and Feighn, M. Kapovich asked whether one can extend this combination theorem for graphs of groups to complexes of groups (see
[
Kap08
, Problem
90
]
). (For more detailed exposition in complexes of groups, on is referred to
[
SG91
]
,
[
Hae92
]
,
[
Cor92
]
,
[
BH99
]
.) One may formulate the problem of M. Kapovich as follows.

Problem 1.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is a developable complex of groups over a finite connected simplicial complex
\mathcal{Y}
such that the following holds.

(1)

All the local groups are hyperbolic.

(2)

All the local maps are qi embeddings.

(3)

The universal cover of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is hyperbolic.

Under what condition(s) the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
is hyperbolic.

Here is brief history of the activities around this problem. Suppose
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is a complex of groups with the condition as in Problem
1.1
. If
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is negatively curved and all the local groups are finite then
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
is hyperbolic due to Gersten-Stallings (
[
SG91
]
). If the local maps are all isomorphisms onto finite index subgroups of the target groups, local groups are non-elementary hyperbolic and
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
satisfies Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flaring condition then it follows from the work of Mj-Sardar (
[
MS12
]
) that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
is hyperbolic. If the universal cover of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is CAT
(0)
and hyperbolic and the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
on the universal cover is acylindrical then
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
is hyperbolic and local groups are quasiconvex in
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y}))
due to A. Martin (
[
Mar14
]
). Apart from these extreme cases nothing is known. However, in this article, we attempt this question for yet another type of complexes of groups. Let us first outline the setup. We refer this as
setup
\bm{\mathcal{C}}

(1)

Suppose
Y
is a finite connected graph and
p_{Y}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow Y
is a graph of spaces where the edge spaces are points. We further assume that
\mathcal{Y}
is a simplicial complex. Suppose
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
is a complex of groups over
\mathcal{Y}
such that all the properties of Problem
1.1
hold with the following additional ones.

(2)

For all
v\in V(Y)
,
p_{Y}^{-1}(v)=\mathcal{Y}_{v}
, say, is a finite connected simplicial complex and the restriction of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
on
\mathcal{Y}_{v}
is a developable complex of groups, say,
\mathcal{G}_{v}(\mathcal{Y}_{v})
over
\mathcal{Y}_{v}
. Further, all the local maps in
\mathcal{G}_{v}(\mathcal{Y}_{v})
are isomorphisms onto finite index subgroups of the target groups.

(3)

Suppose
u,v
are two vertices in
\mathcal{Y}
such that
p_{Y}
is injective when restricted to the edge
e
joining
u,v
. Then the local homomorphisms
G_{e}\rightarrow G_{u}
and
G_{e}\rightarrow G_{v}
are not necessarily isomorphisms onto finite index subgroups.

We denote
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
in this case as
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y)
to emphasize on the extra structure on
\mathcal{Y}
.

Then we have the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

In addition, if
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y)
satisfies Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flare condition and in
(2)
of setup
\mathcal{C}
, all the local groups of
\mathcal{G}_{v}(\mathcal{Y}_{v})
are non-elementary (hyperbolic) then
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y))
is hyperbolic.

The above Theorem
1.2
follows from a combination theorem for spaces. We now elaborate on this. In
[
BF92
]
, Bestvina and Feighn proved that a tree of hyperbolic metric spaces is hyperbolic if it satisfy the qi embedded condition and hallway flaring condition. In
[
MS12
]
, Mj and Sardar proved that if
X
is a metric bundle over
B
such that (1) fibers are uniformly hyperbolic and
B
is also hyperbolic, (2) the barycenter map for the fibers are uniformly coarsely surjective and (3) Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flaring condition holds then
X
is hyperbolic. The question that motivated us is
if we can combine these two and still get hyperbolicity. Here is a baby version of the problem we are attempting to solve.

Question 1.3
.

Suppose
\pi_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow B_{i}
are metric bundles for
i=1,2
. Suppose we join
b_{1}\in B_{1}
and
b_{2}\in B_{2}
by an edge, say,
e
. Let
X_{e}
be a new geodesic metric space. Let
F_{b_{1}}
and
F_{b_{2}}
be fibers over
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
of the bundles
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
respectively. Suppose there are qi embeddings
X_{e}\rightarrow F_{b_{1}}
and
X_{e}\rightarrow F_{b_{2}}
and we form a new space by gluing
X_{e}\times[0,1]
to
X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
as follows: We attach
X_{e}\times\{0\}
to
F_{b_{1}}
and
X_{e}\times\{1\}
to
F_{b_{2}}
using the qi embeddings
X_{e}\rightarrow F_{b_{1}}
and
X_{e}\rightarrow F_{b_{2}}
respectively. When is the new space hyperbolic?

In this article, we consider a general version of Question
1.3
and provide a combination theorem (Theorem
1.4
). One is referred to Definition
5.2
for trees of metric bundles.

Theorem 1.4
.

Suppose
(X,B,T)
is a tree of metric bundles such that:

(1)

For
v\in V(T)\textrm{ and }a\in B_{v}
, the fibers,
F_{a,v}
are uniformly hyperbolic geodesic metric spaces and the barycenter maps
\partial^{3}F_{a,v}\rightarrow F_{a,v}
are uniformly coarsely surjective.

(2)

Let
[v,w]
be an edge in
T
and
\mathfrak{e}=[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}]
be the edge joining
\mathfrak{v}\in B_{v}
and
\mathfrak{w}\in B_{w}
. Then
\pi_{X}
restricted to
\pi_{X}^{-1}(\mathfrak{e})
is a tree of metric spaces with (uniform) qi embedded condition over
\mathfrak{e}=[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}]
.

(3)

B
is hyperbolic geodesic metric space.

(4)

Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flaring condition is satisfied.

Then
X
is hyperbolic geodesic metric space.

Remark 1.5
.

The overall idea of the proof of Theorem
1.4
closely follows from that of
[
KS22
]
and makes crucial use of
[
MS12
]
. We are intellectually indebted to both of these works. However it is not a direct consequence of the combination theorems of
[
BF92
]
and
[
MS12
]
in any obvious way for the following reason. Let
v\in V(T)
. As the space
X_{v}
over
B_{v}
is a metric bundle satisfying all conditions of the main theorem of
[
MS12
]
,
X_{v}
is (uniformly) hyperbolic. Now we can think of
X
as a tree of metric spaces over
T
where vertex spaces are these bundles and the edge spaces are inverse images under
\pi_{B}\circ\pi_{X}
of the midpoints of the edges in
T
. But we can not apply the main theorem of
[
BF92
]
to this tree of spaces to conclude our theorem because in this case the edge spaces of the tree of spaces are not, in general, qi embedded in the corresponding vertex spaces.

We also prove the following combination theorem for trees of metric spaces within an axiomatic framework. One is referred to Section
4
for the hypotheses
(\mathcal{P}0)-(\mathcal{P}4)
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Suppose
\pi:X\rightarrow T
is a tree of metric spaces satisfying properties
(\mathcal{P}0)-(\mathcal{P}4)
. Then
X
is hyperbolic metric space.

Necessity of flaring

Gersten (see
[
Ger98
, Corollary
6.7
]
) showed that the annuli flaring is necessary for the fundamental group of a finite graph of hyperbolic groups to be hyperbolic provided the edge groups are qi embedded in the corresponding vertex groups. Mj and Sardar also showed that the (hallway) flaring condition is necessary for metric bundles to be hyperbolic provided fibers are uniformly hyperbolic (see
[
MS12
, Proposition
5.8
]
). Let us briefly recall the idea of their proof. They first showed that small girth ladders bounded by two qi lifts satisfy flaring condition. Then a general ladder was subdivided into small girth ladders and summing them up showed that a general ladder satisfies flaring condition. In doing so they used a crucial lemma (
[
MS12
, Lemma
5.9
]
) which is a specialization of the fact that geodesics diverge exponentially in hyperbolic metric spaces in the context of metric bundles; this lemma also holds true in trees of metric bundles. In trees of metric bundles, given two qi lifts over the same base, there is a special ladder (see Definition
5.11
) bounded by these qi lifts (see Lemma
5.14
). Therefore, the proof of the following remark is analogous to that of
[
MS12
, Proposition
5.8
]
, so we omit the full details.

Remark 1.7
.

Suppose
(X,B,T)
is a tree of metric bundles such that:

(1)

X
is hyperbolic.

(2)

All the fibers are uniformly hyperbolic.

(3)

All the edge spaces in the corresponding fibers are uniformly qi embedded.

Then
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow B
satisfies Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flaring condition.

As a consequence of Remark
1.7
, we have the following.

Corollary 1.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y)
is a complex of groups as explained in the setup
\mathcal{C}
such that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y))
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y)
is hyperbolic. Then
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y},Y)
satisfies Bestvina-Feighn’s hallway flare condition. (Note that we do not require the universal cover of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
to be hyperbolic.)

A few words on the proof of Theorem
1.4
and organization of the paper:

(0) In Section
2
and Section
3
, we recall basics definitions and results which are used in the subsequent sections. We define trees of metric bundle and discuss some of their properties in Section
5
. Section
4
is devoted to proving Theorem
1.6
. One is not required Section
4
to study Section
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
but for Section
10
.

(1) Motivated by that of
[
KS22
]
, we construct semicontinuous families, ladders and flow spaces, and more general flow spaces in Section
6
; whereas ladder was invented by Mitra in
[
Mit98b
]
for trees of metric spaces. Some properties of these subspaces, most importantly, Mitra’s retraction of the whole space on these subspaces, are also discussed there. Main construction starts from here in Section
6
.

(2) Most difficult job was to show the uniform hyperbolicity of ladders and flow spaces. In Section
7
, we prove that the ladders are (uniformly) hyperbolic by
dividing into two cases: small girth and general case. By invoking Bowditch criterion (see Proposition
2.10
) for a metric space to be hyperbolic, we prove that small girth ladders are (uniformly) hyperbolic (Subsection
7.1
). For general ladder, we first break it up into small girth ladder and then with the help of Proposition
2.11
we conclude its hyperbolicity (Subsection
7.2
). Section
8
is devoted to prove the (uniform) hyperbolicity of flow spaces (Theorem
8.16
). To prove this, we follow the strategy elaborated in
[
KS22
, Chapter
5
]
.

(3) In Section
9
, we prove that union of uniform neighborhood of two intersecting flow spaces is uniformly hyperbolic (Proposition
9.7
); in the introduction of this section we elaborate what the properties are required from the earlier sections to prove this.

(4) Following is the content of Section
10
. Now we think the tree of metric bundles
(X,B,T)
as a tree of metric spaces
\pi:X\rightarrow T
(Remark
1.5
). Then using the results we have proved in earlier sections, we verify the properties
(\mathcal{P}0)-(\mathcal{P}4)
of Theorem
1.6
; where the flow spaces of metric bundles serve as the specified subspaces as in properties
(\mathcal{P}0)-(\mathcal{P}4)
. Whence Theorem
1.4
follows.

(5) Section
11
contains some applications to complexes of groups (Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.8
).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Some basic concepts

We are assuming that the reader is familiar with the basic notions of large-scale geometry (
[
Gd90
,
Gro87
,
JAS91
]
). However, we will recall some of these for later use. Let
(X,d_{X}),(Y,d_{Y})
be metric spaces and
\epsilon\geq 0,r\geq 0,C\geq 0,D\geq 0,R\geq 0
. A subset
A
of
X
is said to be
r
-
dense
if
X=N_{r}(A):=\{x\in X:d_{X}(x,A)\leq r\}
. Let
A,B\subseteq X
. We say
A
and
B
are
R
-
separated
if
d_{X}(a,b)>R,\forall~a\in A
and
\forall~b\in B
. Suppose
U,V\subseteq X
are closed subsets. We say the pair
(U,V)
is
D
-
cobounded
in
X
if max
\{diam(P_{X,U}(V)),diam(P_{X,V}(U))\}\leq D
, where
P_{X,U}(V)
is the set of all nearest point projections of points of
V
on
U
and
P_{X,V}(U)
is that of points of
U
on
V
(see Subsection
2.3
). A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is called
\epsilon
-
coarsely surjective
if
N_{\epsilon}(f(X))=Y
; and we say
f
is
coarsely surjective
if it is
\epsilon
-coarsely surjective for some
\epsilon
. A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is called
C
-
coarsely Lipschitz
if
d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq Cd_{X}(x,x^{\prime})+C
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
. In particular, if
A\subseteq X
and there is a map
g:X\rightarrow A
such that
d_{X}(g(x),g(x^{\prime}))\leq Cd_{X}(x,x^{\prime})+C
,
\forall~x,x^{\prime}\in X
and
g(a)=a
,
\forall~a\in A
then we call
g
a
C
-
coarsely Lipschitz retraction
of
X
on
A
. A function
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is said to be
proper
if
\phi(r)\rightarrow\infty
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. Then we say a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is
metrically proper
as measured by some proper function
\phi
if
d_{Y}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq r
implies
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\leq\phi(r)
. In this case, we usually say
f
is
\phi
-proper embedding.

For
x,y\in X
, a
geodesic
joining them in
X
is an isometric embedding
\alpha:[0,d_{X}(x,y)]\rightarrow X
with
\alpha(0)=x
and
\alpha(d_{X}(x,y))=y
. We refer to X as a
geodesic metric space
if there exists a geodesic in
X
joining every pair of points in
X
. A
tree
is a connected graph (see
[
BH99
, Section
1.9
, I
.1
]
) without any embedded circle. (Here all the edges are isometric to a closed unit interval of
\mathbb{R}
.) For a tree
T
and
u,v\in V(T)
, by a
segment
or
interval
joining
u,v
in
T
, we mean an isometric embedding
\alpha:[n,m]\rightarrow T
for
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\alpha(n)=u
,
\alpha(m)=v
. We denote
[u,v]:=Im(\alpha)
.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space. We denote a geodesic in
X
joining two points
a,b\in X
by
[a,b]\subseteq X
. Suppose
x,y,z\in X
. A
geodesic triangle
in
X
formed by these three points is the union of chosen geodesic segments
[x,y],[x,z]
and
[y,z]
, and it is denoted by
\triangle(x,y,z)
. We call those geodesic segments as sides of the triangle. We say
\triangle(x,y,z)
is
\delta
-
slim
if any side is contained in the
\delta
-neighborhood of the union of other two sides. Suppose
\triangle(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is a geodesic triangle in
X
where
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in X
. A point
z\in X
is called
C
-
center
of this triangle if
z\in N_{C}([x_{i},x_{j}])
for
i\neq j
and
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Sometimes we call
\cup_{i=1}^{3}[z,x_{i}]
a
C
-
tripod
in
X
with end points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
. Let
a,a^{\prime}\in X
. A
discrete path
joining
a
and
a^{\prime}
in
X
is a finite set of points in an order, say,
a=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}=a^{\prime}
. A path joining
a
and
a^{\prime}
based on a discrete path above is
[a_{0},a_{1}]\cup[a_{1},a_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{n-1},a_{n}]
.

The following lemmata (Lemma
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
) are standard. So we omit the proofs.

Lemma 2.1
.

Given
D\geq 0
there is
C_{\ref{imp-coarse-retraction}}(D)
such that the following holds.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
Y
is a subset of
X
(not necessarily connected) such that
Y
is
1
-dense in
X
. Let
U\subseteq X
and
\rho:Y\rightarrow U
be a map such that
d_{X}(\rho(x),\rho(y))\leq D
for all
x,y\in Y
with
d_{X}(x,y)\leq 1
. Then
\rho
can be extended to a map
\rho^{\prime}:X\rightarrow U
so that
\rho^{\prime}
is
C_{\ref{imp-coarse-retraction}}
-coarsely Lipschitz.

Lemma 2.2
.

Given a map
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and constants
C>0
,
R\geq 0
there is a constant
L_{\ref{lrape_qi-emb}}=L_{\ref{lrape_qi-emb}}(\phi,C,R)
such that we have the following.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
Y\subseteq X
such that
N_{R}(Y)
is path connected. Let
p:X\rightarrow Y
be a
C
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction. Further, the inclusion
i:N_{R}(Y)\hookrightarrow X
is
\phi
-proper embedding. Then
i:N_{R}(Y)\hookrightarrow X
is
L_{\ref{lrape_qi-emb}}
-qi embedding. We consider
N_{R}(Y)
with its induced path metric from
X
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Given
L\geq 1,~D\geq 0
, there is a constant
L_{\ref{hd-imp-qi}}=L_{\ref{hd-imp-qi}}(L,D)
such that we have following.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
Y\subseteq Z\subseteq X
are geodesic subspaces such that the inclusion
Y\hookrightarrow X
is
L
-qi embedding. Let
Z\subseteq N_{D}(Y)
. Then the inclusion
Z\hookrightarrow X
is
L_{\ref{hd-imp-qi}}
-qi embedding.

2.2.
Hyperbolic metric spaces

There are several equivalent definitions for hyperbolic geodesic metric spaces (see
[
Sho91
]
,
[
Gro87
]
).
We consider the following and refer this as Gromov hyperbolic space.

Definition 2.4
.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
\delta\geq 0
. We say that
X
is
\delta
-hyperbolic if all its geodesic triangles are
\delta
-slim.

A geodesic metric space
X
is said to be hyperbolic if it is
\delta
-hyperbolic for some
\delta\geq 0
.

For us hyperbolic metric spaces are geodesic (by definition above) and are of infinite diameter. In hyperbolic metric spaces, quasi-geodesics and geodesics with same end points are uniformly Hausdorff close. This is known as Morse lemma or stability of quasi-geodesic (see
[
BH99
, Theorem
1.7
, III.H]
).

Lemma 2.5

(Stability of quasi-geodesic)
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~k\geq 1
and
\epsilon\geq 0
there is a constant
D_{\ref{ml}}=D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,k,\epsilon)
such that the following holds.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space. Then for any geodesic
\alpha
and a
(k,\epsilon)
-quasi-geodesic
\beta
in
X
with the same end points,
Hd_{X}(\alpha,\beta)\leq D_{\ref{ml}}
.

The barycenter map
(For more details, one is referred to
[
MS12
, Section
2
]
): Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic geodesic metric space such that there are more than two elements in its Gromov boundary,
\partial X
. Then by
[
MS12
, Lemma
2.4
]
, for any
\eta,\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}\in\partial X
such that
\eta^{\prime}\neq\eta^{\prime\prime}
, there is a (uniform) quasi-geodesic ray starting at any point in
X
representing
\eta
and a (uniform) bi-infinite quasi-geodesic line whose one end represents
\eta^{\prime}
and the other one represents
\eta^{\prime\prime}
. We denote such a line by
(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
. Notice that we do not assume our space to be proper. Let
\partial^{3}X=\{(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in\partial X\times\partial X\times\partial X:\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}\neq\xi_{3}\neq\xi_{1}\}
. Now for
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in\partial^{3}X
, we consider an ideal quasi-geodesic triangle, say,
\triangle(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
formed by three (uniform) quasi-geodesic lines
\{(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}):i\neq j\textrm{ and }i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\}
. Then by
[
MS12
, Lemma
2.7
]
, there is a point, say,
b_{\xi}
in
X
uniformly close to each sides of
\triangle(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
and this
b_{\xi}
is coarsely well-defined. Thus in this way, we can (coarsely) define a map
\psi:\partial^{3}X\rightarrow X
. Lastly, by
[
MS12
, Lemma
2.9
]
, the map
\psi:\partial^{3}X\rightarrow X
is coarsely unique and is called
the barycenter map
. Note that for such barycenter map, we always assume that
\partial X
has more than two elements.

For a finitely generated group
G
with finite generating set
S
, the Cayley graph of
G
with respect to
S
is a graph whose vertex set is
G
and two vertices, say,
g,h\in G
are joined by an edge if
g^{-1}h\in S\cup S^{-1}
.

Definition 2.6

(
Hyperbolic group).

A finitely generated group
G
is said to be hyperbolic if its Cayley graph with respect to some finite generating set is hyperbolic.

We say a group
G
is non-elementary hyperbolic if the Gromov boundary of its Cayley graph with respect to some finite generating set contains more than two elements.

It is standard that given two finite generating sets, the Cayley graphs associated with them become quasi-isometric to each other. It is easy to prove from the stability of quasi-geodesic (Lemma
2.5
) that the hyperbolicity is quasi-isometry invariant (see
[
BH99
, Theorem
1.9
, III.H]
). Therefore, hyperbolic groups are well-defined.

Remark 2.7
.

It is a well known fact that for a non-elementary hyperbolic group the barycenter map is coarsely surjective.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
\alpha:[s,t]\subseteq\mathbb{R}\rightarrow X
is a continuous injective path. Let
d_{\alpha}
be the induced path metric on
Im(\alpha)
from
X
. Then we have the induced order on
Im(\alpha)
from
[s,t]
. In other words, if
p,q\in[s,t]
with
p\leq q
then
\alpha(p)\leq\alpha(q)
keeping in mind that
d_{\alpha}(\alpha(s),\alpha(p))\leq d_{\alpha}(\alpha(s),\alpha(q))
. We have mentioned above that sometimes we forget the domain of quasi-geodesic (resp. geodesic) and work with their image. With this terminology, we have the following.

Lemma 2.8
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~k\geq 1
and
r\geq 0
, we have constants
L_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}=L_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}(\delta,k,r)
and
k_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}=k_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}(\delta,k,r)
such that the following hold.

Suppose
(X,d)
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space. Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be continuous injective
k
-quasi-geodesics in
X
joining points
a_{1},a_{2}
and
b_{1},b_{2}
respectively. Further, we suppose that
d(a_{i},b_{i})\leq r,~i=1,2
. Let
a_{1}\leq a_{2}
and
b_{1}\leq b_{2}
be the orders on
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively. Then there is a monotonic (piece-wise linear) homeomorphism
\psi:(\alpha,d_{\alpha})\rightarrow(\beta,d_{\beta})
such that
\psi(a_{i})=b_{i}
and
d(x,\psi(x))\leq k_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}
for all
x\in\alpha
. Moreover,
\psi
is
L_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}
-quasi-isometry.

Proof.

Define a map
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\phi(t)=kt+k^{2}
. Then
\alpha,\beta
are
\phi
-properly embedded. Now by Lemma
2.5
and
\delta
-slimness of geodesic triangles, we have
Hd(\alpha,\beta)\leq D_{1}
, where
D_{1}=2D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,k,k)+2\delta+r
. Thus by
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.19
]
, we have a map
g:\alpha\rightarrow\beta
with
d(g(x),x)\leq D_{1},\forall~x\in\alpha\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
g(a_{i})=b_{i},i=1,2
such that
g
is
L
-quasi-isometry, where
L
depends on
D_{1}
and
\phi
. Again, by
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.24
]
, we have constants
D_{2},D_{3}
depending on
L
, and a monotonic (piece-wise linear) homeomorphism
\tilde{g}:\alpha\rightarrow\beta
such that
\tilde{g}
is
D_{2}
-quasi-isometry and
d(g(x),\tilde{g}(x))\leq D_{3}
. So
d(x,\tilde{g}(x))\leq d(x,g(x))+d(g(x),\tilde{g}(x))\leq D_{1}+D_{3}
. Here
\tilde{g}
serves as the required
\psi
.

Therefore, we can take
L_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}=D_{2}
and
k_{\ref{monotonic-map-between-qgs}}=D_{1}+D_{3}
.
∎

We end this subsection by stating the following results (Lemma
2.9
, Proposition
2.10
and Proposition
2.11
). One can look at
[
CDP90
, Theorem
1.4
, Chapter
3
]
for a proof of Lemma
2.9
.

Lemma 2.9

(Local quasi-geodesic vs global quasi-geodesic)
.

For all
\delta\geq 0,~k\geq 1
and
\epsilon\geq 0
there are constants
L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}=L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,k,\epsilon)
and
\lambda_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}=\lambda_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,k,\epsilon)
such that the following holds.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space. Then any
(k,\epsilon,L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}})
-local quasi-geodesic in
X
is a
\lambda_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}
-quasi-geodesic.

In
[
Bow14
, Proposition
3.1
]
, Bowditch provided a criterion for hyperbolicity of a metric graph. Earlier, in
[
Ham07
]
, Hamenstadt also gave a similar criterion for a space to be hyperbolic. In Proposition
2.10
, we consider Bowditch’s version for space.

Proposition 2.10
.

(
[
KS22
, Corollary
1.63
]
)

Given
D_{0}\geq 1,~D\geq 0
and a proper map
\psi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
there exist
\delta_{\ref{combing}}=\delta_{\ref{combing}}(\psi,D,D_{0})
and
K_{\ref{combing}}=K_{\ref{combing}}(\psi,D,D_{0})
such that the following holds.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
X_{0}\subseteq X
is a
D_{0}
-dense subset of
X
. Suppose for any pair
(x,y)
of distinct points in
X_{0}
there is a continuous path
c(x,y)
joining
x
and
y
. Further, for all
x,y,z\in X_{0}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(1)

d(x,y)\leq r
implies the length of the path
c(x,y)
is bounded by
\psi(r)
, and

(2)

c(x,y)\subseteq N_{D}(c(x,z)\cup c(y,z))
.

Then
X
is
\delta_{\ref{combing}}
-hyperbolic metric space and the paths
c(x,y)
are
K_{\ref{combing}}
-quasi-geodesic.

The proposition below is a very special case of the main theorem of
[
BF92
]
(see also
[
KS22
, Theorem
2.59
]
). Here, the space is realized as a tree of metric spaces such that the tree is an interval. (One may look at
[
MS12
, Corollary
1.52
]
for this result in metric graph.) However, it is true for an arbitrary tree also (see
[
PP22
, Theorem
2
]
).

Proposition 2.11
.

(
[
KS22
, Theorem
2.59
]
)

Given
\delta\geq 0,~L\geq 1,~D\geq 0
there exists
\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps}}=\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps}}(\delta,L,D)
such that the following holds.

Suppose
X=\cup_{i=0}^{n-1}X_{i}
is a geodesic metric space with
X_{i}
’s are geodesic subspaces with the induced path metric from
X
such that:

(1)

For
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
X_{i}
is
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space.

(2)

For
0\leq i\leq n-2
,
Y_{i+1}=X_{i}\cap X_{i+1}
is a path connected subspace, and the inclusions
Y_{i+1}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
and
Y_{i+1}\hookrightarrow X_{i+1}
are
L
-qi embeddings.

(3)

Y_{i+1}
separates
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
in
X
in the sense that every path in
X
joining points in
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
passes through
Y_{i+1}
.

(4)

For
1\leq i\leq n-2
, the pair
(Y_{i},Y_{i+1})
is
D
-cobounded in the metric
X_{i}
.

(5)

d_{X_{i}}(Y_{i},Y_{i+1})\geq 1
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

Then
X
is
\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps}}
-hyperbolic metric space.

Remark 2.12
.

In Proposition
2.11
, if
n=2
, we only need to check
(1)
and
(2)
. In that case,
X
is
\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps-2}}=\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps-2}}(\delta,L)
-hyperbolic (see also Lemma
3.3
).

2.3.
Quasiconvex subsets

In this subsection, we will explore various basic results concerning quasiconvex subsets that will be useful in later discussions.

Definition 2.13
.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
K\geq 0
. A subset
U
of
X
is said to be
K
-
quasiconvex
if
[a,b]\subseteq N_{K}(U)
for all
a,b\in U
and for all geodesics
[a,b]
joining
a,b
in
X
. We say that a subset
U
of
X
is quasiconvex if it is
K
-quasiconvex for some
K\geq 0
.

In hyperbolic geodesic metric space, a common example of quasiconvex subset is the convex hull of any subset. This motivates us to define the following.

Definition 2.14
.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
U\subseteq X
. The
quasiconvex hull
of
U
is defined as hull
(U):=\{[a,b]:a,b\in U\}
.

Remark 2.15
.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space for some
\delta\geq 0
and
A\subset X
is any subset. Then hull
(A)
is
2\delta
-quasiconvex.

In hyperbolic metric space
X
, nearest point projection of a point on a quasiconvex subset, say,
U
is coarsely well-defined. (Here one requires
U
to be closed; which is the standard assumption for us for a quasiconvex subset.) We define a map
P_{X,U}:X\rightarrow U
sending a point to its nearest point projection, called
a nearest point projection map
on
U
. Sometimes we denote this map by
P_{U}
if
X
is understood. Now we collect some facts related to quasiconvex subsets; some are well known and some are very easy to prove.

Lemma 2.16
.

(
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.139
, Lemma
1.127
]
,
[
MS12
, Lemma
1.35
]
)

Given
\delta\geq 0,~\lambda\geq 0
and
R\geq 0
, we have
R_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=R_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}(\delta,\lambda)=2\lambda+5\delta
,
D_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=D_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}(\delta,\lambda)=2\lambda+7\delta
and
R^{\prime}_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=2\lambda+3\delta+R
such that the following hold.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space. Let
Y,~Z\subseteq X
be two
\lambda
-quasiconvex subsets in
X
. Then we have the following.

(1)

If
Y,~Z
are
R_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}
-separated then the pair
(Y,Z)
is
D_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}
-cobounded.

(2)

If
d(Y,Z)\leq R
then
P_{Y}(Z)\subseteq N_{R^{\prime}_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}}(Z)\cap Y
and
Hd(P_{Y}(Z),P_{Z}(Y))\leq R^{\prime}_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}
.

One is referred to
[
KS22
, Remark
1.142
]
for the upcoming remark. It is possible to minimize those constants, similar to what is mentioned in the remark.

Remark 2.17
.

(1)

If
Y
and
Z
are geodesic segments in Lemma
2.16

(1)
, then one can take
D_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=8\delta
and
R_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=5\delta
.

(2)

If
Y
and
Z
are geodesic segments in Lemma
2.16

(2)
, then one can take
R^{\prime}_{\ref{R-sep-D-cobdd}}=4\delta+R
.

Lemma 2.18
.

(
[
KS22
, Corollary
1.140
(a)]
)

Let
\delta\geq 0
,
k\geq 0
and
D\geq 0
. Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space, and
U
and
V
are
k
-quasiconvex subsets of
X
. Further, we assume that diam
\{P_{X,U}(V)\}\leq D
. Then there is a constant
D^{\prime}\geq D
depending on
\delta,k
and
D
such that diam
\{P_{X,V}(U)\}\leq D^{\prime}
. In particular, the pair
(U,V)
is
D^{\prime}
-cobounded.

Lemma 2.19
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~k\geq 1,~\lambda\geq 0
and
D\geq 0
there is a constant
C_{\ref{cobounded-pairs}}=C_{\ref{cobounded-pairs}}(\delta,k,\lambda,D)
such that the following holds.

Let
X
be a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space. Suppose
U
is a
\lambda
-quasiconvex in
X
and
x,y\in X
such that
d_{X}(P_{U}(x),P_{U}(y))\leq D
. Let
\alpha
be a
k
-quasi-geodesic in
X
joining
x
and
y
. Then the pair
(\alpha,U)
is
C_{\ref{cobounded-pairs}}
-cobounded.

Proof.

Since quasi-geodesics are quasiconvex subsets, so the lemma follows from
[
KS22
, Lemma 1.113]
and Lemma
2.18
.
∎

One is referred to
[
KS22
, Corollary 1.105]
for a proof of Lemma
2.20
(2), and (3) easily follows from (1) and (2), so we omit the proof.

Lemma 2.20
.

Given
\delta\geq 0
,
K\geq 0
,
D\geq 0
and
R\geq 0
there are constants
C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}=C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta,K)
,
E_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}=E_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta,K,D)
and
D_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}=D_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta,K,D,R)
such that the following hold.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space, and
U
and
V
are
K
-quasiconvex subsets of
X
. Then we have the following.

(1)

(
[
Gro87
, Hyperbolic Groups, Lemma
7.3.D
]
)
Any nearest point projection map
P_{X,U}:X\rightarrow U
is
C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction.

(2)

Suppose
x\in X
and
Hd(U,V)\leq D
. If
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are nearest point projections of
x
on
U
and
V
respectively, then
d(x_{1},x_{2})\leq E_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}
.

(3)

Suppose the pair
(U,V)
is
D
-cobounded. Then
N_{R}(U)
and
N_{R}(V)
are
K_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}
-quasiconvex, and the pair
(N_{R}(U),N_{R}(V))
is
D_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}
-cobounded.

Lemma 2.21
.

For
\delta\geq 0,~K\geq 0
and
L\geq 1
, we have
K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta,L,K)
and
D_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}=D_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta,L,K)
such that the following hold.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are
\delta
-hyperbolic metric spaces, and
f:Y\rightarrow X
is a
L
-qi embedding. Let
U
be a
K
-quasiconvex subset of
Y
and
y\in Y
. Then we have the following.

(1)

f(U)
is
K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}
-quasiconvex in
X
. (For this, we do not need
Y
to be hyperbolic.)

(2)

(
[
Mit98b
, Lemma
3.5
]
)
If
y^{\prime}
is a nearest point projection of
y
on
U
in
Y
and
x^{\prime}
is that of
f(y)
on
f(U)
in
X
. Then
d_{Y}(f(y^{\prime}),x^{\prime})\leq D_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}
.

We saw that qi embedded subspaces are quasiconvex in hyperbolic metric spaces in Lemma
2.21

(1)
. Now by Lemma
2.20

(1)
one can conclude Lemma
2.22
for a converse.

Lemma 2.22
.

Given a map
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and constants
\delta\geq 0
,
k\geq 0
and
R\geq k+1
there are constants
L_{\ref{qi-emb-in-Y-X}}=L_{\ref{qi-emb-in-Y-X}}(\delta,k,R)
and
L^{\prime}_{\ref{qi-emb-in-Y-X}}=L^{\prime}_{\ref{qi-emb-in-Y-X}}(\delta,k,R,\phi)
such that we have the following.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space and
A
is a
k
-quasiconvex subset of
X
. Then
N^{X}_{R}(A)
is path connected and with its induced path metric from
X
, the inclusion
N^{X}_{R}(A)\hookrightarrow X
is
L_{\ref{qi-emb-in-Y-X}}
-qi embedding.

Lemma 2.23
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~L\geq 1
and
K\geq 0
, we have constants

K_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}=K_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta,L,K)\textrm{ and }D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}=D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta,L,K)

such that the following holds.

Suppose
X
is
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space and
Y\subseteq X
is a geodesic subspace such that the inclusion
i:(Y,d_{Y})\hookrightarrow(X,d_{X})
is
L
-qi embedding where
d_{Y}
is the induced path metric on
Y
from
X
. Let
A\subseteq Y
be
K
-quasiconvex in
Y
. Further, we assume that
y\in Y
, and
y^{\prime}
is a nearest point projection of
y
on
A
in the metric
Y
and
y^{\prime\prime}
is that of
y
on
A
in the metric
X
. Then
A
is
K_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}
-quasiconvex in
X
and
d_{X}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\leq D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.21

(1)
, one can take
K_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta,L,K)
.

For the second part, by
[
MS12
, Lemma
1.31
(2)]
, we note that the arc-length parametrization of
[y,y^{\prime}]_{Y}\cup[y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}]_{Y}
is a
(3+2K)
-quasi-geodesic in
Y
and so is
L_{1}
-quasi-geodesic in
X
for some constant
L_{1}
depending on
(3+2K)
and
L
. Suppose
y_{1}\in[y,y^{\prime\prime}]_{X}
such that
d_{X}(y^{\prime},y_{1})\leq D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,L_{1},L_{1})
, and so
d_{X}(y_{1},y^{\prime\prime})\leq D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,L_{1},L_{1})
. Hence
d_{X}(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{X}(y^{\prime},y_{1})+d_{X}(y_{1},y^{\prime\prime})\leq 2D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,L_{1},L_{1})=:D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}
.
∎

Here we recall from
[
KS22
, Section
1.18
]
, a small modification in nearest point projection on a path connected quasiconvex subset (
[
KS22
, Definition
1.121
]
).

Definition 2.24

(Modified projection)
.

Suppose
X
is a geodesic metric space and
U
is a path connected quasiconvex subset of
X
. Then for any subset
A\subseteq X
, modified projection of
A
on
U
is defined as
\bar{P}_{U}(A):=\textrm{hull}(P_{U}(A))\subseteq U
, where the quasiconvex hull is taken in the induced path metric on
U
from
X
(see Definition
2.14
for notation).

Lemma 2.25
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~L\geq 1
and
\lambda\geq 0
there are constants
\theta_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}=\theta_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}(\delta,L,\lambda)
and
D_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}=D_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}(\delta,L,\lambda)
such that the following hold.

Suppose
X
is a
\delta
-hyperbolic metric space and
Z\subseteq X
such that
Z
with the induced path metric is
L
-qi embedded in
X
. Let
Z
be also
\delta
-hyperbolic. Suppose
x_{i}\in Z
(
i=1,2,3
) and
z
is a
\delta
-center of the triangle
\triangle(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in
Z
giving a
\delta
-tripod
Y=\cup_{i=1}^{3}[z,x_{i}]_{Z}
in
Z
. Further, we assume that
U
is a
\lambda
-quasiconvex subset of
X
. Let
Y
be
\lambda
-quasiconvex in
X
. Let
P_{Y}:X\rightarrow Y
and
P_{U}:X\rightarrow U
be nearest point projection maps on
Y
and on
U
respectively. Then:

(1)

Hd(P_{Y}(U),\bar{P}_{Y}(U))\leq\theta_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}(\delta,L,\lambda)
.

(2)

Let
\bar{Y}=\bar{P}_{Y}(U)
and
\bar{x}_{i}\in\bar{Y}
be the closest to
x_{i}
in the intrinsic path metric on
Y
. Then:

(a)

Let
\bar{Y}\nsubseteq[z,x_{i}]_{Z}
for any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then
d_{X}(P_{U}(x_{i}),P_{U}(\bar{x}_{i}))\leq D_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}
.

(b)

Let
\bar{Y}\subseteq[z,x_{i}]_{Z}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Note that
\bar{x}_{i+1}=\bar{x}_{i-1}=\bar{z}
(say). Here
i\pm 1
is calculated in modulo
3
. Then

d_{X}(P_{U}(\bar{x}_{i}),P_{U}(x_{i})),~d_{X}(P_{U}(z),P_{U}(\bar{z}))\textrm{ and }d_{X}(P_{U}(x_{i\pm 1}),P_{U}(\bar{z}))

are bounded by
D_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}
.

Proof.

The proof of
(1)
follows from that of
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.125
]
. We only proof
(2)

(b)
since the proof for
(2)

(a)
is a line by line argument of that of
(2)

(b)
. In
(2)

(b)
, we will specifically address
d_{X}(P_{U}(x_{i-1}),P_{U}(\bar{z}))
as the other proofs are similar. We fix
i=2
. Then
x_{i-1}=x_{1}
.

Let
P_{U}(x_{1})=x^{\prime}_{1}
and
P_{Y}(x^{\prime}_{1})=x^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. Note that
x^{\prime\prime}_{1}\in[\bar{z},x_{2}]_{Z}
. Since
z
is
\delta
-center of the triangle
\triangle(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in
Z
, so
d_{Z}(\bar{z},[x_{1},x^{\prime\prime}_{1}]_{Z})\leq 2\delta
. Thus (by Lemma
2.5
)
\exists~z_{1}\in[x_{1},x^{\prime\prime}_{1}]_{X}
such that
d_{X}(\bar{z},z_{1})\leq 2\delta+D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,L,L)
. Again,
[x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime\prime}_{1}]_{X}\cup[x^{\prime\prime}_{1},x_{1}]_{X}
is
(3+2\lambda)
-quasi-geodesic in
X
(
[
MS12
, Lemma
1.31

(2)
]
), and so
\exists~z_{2}\in[x_{1},x^{\prime}_{1}]_{X}
such that
d_{X}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,3+2\lambda,3+2\lambda)
. Then by triangle inequality,
d_{X}(\bar{z},z_{2})\leq D
, where
D=D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,3+2\lambda,3+2\lambda)+2\delta+D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,L,L)
. Notice that as
P_{U}(x)=x^{\prime}_{1}
and
z_{2}\in[x_{1},x^{\prime}_{1}]_{X}
, so
x^{\prime}_{1}
is also a nearest point projection of
z_{2}
on
U
in the metric of
X
. Then by Lemma
2.20

(1)
,
d_{X}(P_{U}(z_{2}),x^{\prime}_{1})\leq C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta,\lambda)
. Hence,
d_{X}(P_{U}(\bar{z},x^{\prime}_{1})\leq d(P_{U}(\bar{z}),P_{U}(z_{2}))+d_{X}(P_{U}(z_{2}),x^{\prime}_{1})\leq C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta,\lambda)(D+2)=:D_{\ref{modified-proj-on-qg}}
.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

(
[
KS22
, Remark
1.124
]
)
In the above Lemma
2.25

(1)
, if both
U
and
T
are geodesic segments in
X
, one can bound
Hd(P_{T}(U),\bar{P}_{T}(U))
by
4\delta
.

3.
Trees of metric spaces

The notion of trees of metric spaces was introduced by Bestvina and Feighn in
[
BF92
]
.
A coarsely equivalent definition was given by Mitra in
[
Mit98b
]
. We are going to
adopt the latter definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
T
is a simplicial tree and
X
is a metric space. Then a
1
-Lipschitz surjective map
\pi:X\rightarrow T
is called a
tree of metric spaces
if there is a proper map
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with the following properties:

(1)

For all
v\in V(T)
,
X_{v}:=\pi^{-1}(v)
is a geodesic metric space with the path metric
d_{v}
induced from
X
. Moreover, with respect to these metrics, the inclusion
X_{v}\hookrightarrow X
is
\phi
-proper embedding.

(2)

Suppose
e
is an edge in
T
joining
v,w\in V(T)
and
m_{e}\in T
is the midpoint of this edge. Then
X_{e}:=\pi^{-1}(m_{e})
is a geodesic metric space with respect to the path metric
d_{e}
induced from
X
. Moreover, there is a map
\vartheta_{e}:X_{e}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\pi^{-1}(e)\subseteq X
such that

(a)

\pi\circ\vartheta_{e}
is the projection map onto
[v,w]
.

(b)

\vartheta_{e}
restricted to
X_{e}\times(0,1)
is an isometry onto
\pi^{-1}(int(e))
where
int(e)
denotes the interior of
e
.

(c)

\vartheta_{e}
restricted to
X_{e}\times\{0\}\simeq X_{e}
and
X_{e}\times\{1\}\simeq X_{e}
are
\phi
-proper embeddings from
X_{e}
into
X_{v}
and
X_{w}
respectively with respect to their induced path metrics. Let us denote these restriction maps by
\vartheta_{e,v}
and
\vartheta_{e,w}
respectively.

Moreover, we say
\pi:X\rightarrow T
is a
tree of hyperbolic metric spaces with the qi embedded condition
if additionally we have the following. There is
\delta_{0}\geq 0
and
L_{0}\geq 1
such that
X_{v}
’s are
\delta_{0}
-hyperbolic for all
v\in V(T)
and in
(2)

(c)
,
\vartheta_{e,v}
and
\vartheta_{e,w}
are
L_{0}
-qi embedding.

In the following lemma we see that if we restrict the tree of metric spaces on some subtree then the inclusion map is uniformly properly embedded.

Lemma 3.2
.

(
[
KS22
, Proposition
2.17
]
)

Suppose
\pi:X\rightarrow T
is a tree of metric spaces. Then there is a function
\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}=\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}(\phi):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
depending on
\phi
as in Definition
3.1
such that the following holds.

Let
T^{\prime}
be a subtree of
T
and
X_{T^{\prime}}:=\pi^{-1}(T^{\prime})
. Then with respect to the path metric on
X_{T^{\prime}}
induced from
X
, the inclusion
X_{T^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow X
is
\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}
-proper embedding.

Convention
: Unless otherwise specified, we always refer to the constants
\delta_{0}
and
L_{0}
as in Definition
3.1
for a tree of hyperbolic metric spaces with the qi embedded condition.

Let us fix some secondary constants
\bm{\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},\lambda^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{1}}
in the following Lemma
3.3
and these notations will be used through out the thesis.

Lemma 3.3
.

(
[
KS22
, Corollary
2.62
, Lemma
2.27
]
)

Suppose
\pi:Z\rightarrow[v,w]
is a tree of metric spaces over an edge
e=[v,w]
such that
Z_{v}
,
Z_{w}
are
\delta_{0}
-hyperbolic and
Z_{e}
is
L_{0}
-qi embedded in both
Z_{v}
and
Z_{w}
. Then

(1)

Z
is
\bm{\delta^{\prime}_{0}}
-hyperbolic, and
Z_{v}
and
Z_{w}
are
\bm{L^{\prime}_{0}}
-qi embedded in
Z
.

(2)

Suppose
U
is a
2\delta_{0}
-quasiconvex subset of the fiber
Z_{v}
or
Z_{w}
. Then
U
is
\bm{\lambda^{\prime}_{0}}
-quasiconvex in
Z

(
see Lemma
2.21

(1))
, where
\lambda^{\prime}_{0}=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},2\delta_{0})
. In particular,
Z_{v},Z_{w}
are
\lambda^{\prime}_{0}
-quasiconvex in
Z
. Thus a nearest point projection map
P_{ZZ_{w}}:Z\rightarrow Z_{w}
in the metric
Z
is
\bm{L^{\prime}_{1}}
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction, where
L^{\prime}_{1}=C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},\lambda^{\prime}_{0})
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Given
k\geq 0,~D\geq 0
and
\epsilon\geq 0
there are constants
R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}=R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}(k,D,\epsilon)
and
R^{\prime}_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}=R^{\prime}_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}(k,D)
such that the following holds.

Suppose we have assumptions of Lemma
3.3
. Let
A_{v}
be a
k
-quasiconvex subsets of
Z_{v}
in
Z_{v}
-metric and
A_{w}
be that of
Z_{w}
in
Z_{w}
-metric. Let
x\in Z_{v}
and
y\in Z_{w}
such that
d_{Z}(x,y)=1
, and
x^{\prime}
be a nearest point projection of
x
on
A_{v}
in
Z_{v}
-metric and
y^{\prime}
be that of
y
on
A_{w}
in
Z_{w}
-metric. Further, we assume that
Hd_{Z}(P_{ZZ_{w}}(A_{v}),A_{w})\leq\epsilon
and
d_{Z}(z,A_{v})\leq D
for all
z\in A_{w}
. Then
d_{Z}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}
.

Proof.

Note that
Z
is
\delta^{\prime}_{0}
-hyperbolic, and
Z_{v}
and
Z_{w}
are
L^{\prime}_{0}
-qi embedded in
Z
(see Lemma
3.3
). Thus
A_{v}
and
A_{w}
are
K
-quasiconvex in
Z
for some
K=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},k)
. Suppose
x_{1}
is a nearest point projection of
x
on
A_{v}
and
y_{1}
is that of
y
on
A_{w}
in
Z
. Then by Lemma
2.23
,
d_{Z}(x^{\prime},x_{1})\leq D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},K)
and
d_{Z}(y^{\prime},y_{1})\leq D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},K)
. Hence it is enough to show a bound on
d_{Z}(x_{1},y_{1})
.

Let
y_{2}
and
y_{3}
be nearest point projections of
y
and
y_{1}
on
A_{v}
respectively in
Z
. By given condition we have
d_{Z}(y_{1},y_{3})\leq D
and by Lemma
2.20
(1) we have
d_{Z}(x_{1},y_{2})\leq 2C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},K)
. Consider the pair
(A_{v},Z_{w})
in
Z
. By Lemma
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.127
]
, if
y^{\prime}_{2}
is a nearest point projection of
y_{2}
on
Z_{w}
, we have
d_{Z}(y_{2},y^{\prime}_{2})\leq 2K+3\delta^{\prime}_{0}+D
. Again by given condition we have
d_{Z}(y^{\prime}_{2},A_{w})\leq\epsilon
, and so
d_{Z}(y_{2},A_{w})\leq 2K+3\delta^{\prime}_{0}+D+\epsilon
. Now if
y^{\prime\prime}_{2}
is a nearest point projection of
y_{2}
on
A_{w}
, then
d_{Z}(y_{2},y^{\prime\prime}_{2})\leq 2K+3\delta^{\prime}_{0}+D+\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}
(say).

Now we have
d_{Z}(y,y_{3})\leq d_{Z}(y,y_{1})+d_{Z}(y_{1},y_{3})\leq d_{Z}(y,y^{\prime\prime}_{2})+d_{Z}(y_{1},y_{3})\leq d_{Z}(y,y_{2})\newline
+d_{Z}(y_{2},y^{\prime\prime}_{2})+D\leq d_{Z}(y,y_{2})+\epsilon^{\prime}+D
. Let
z\in[y,y_{3}]_{Z}
such that
d_{Z}(y_{2},z)\leq K+2\delta_{0}^{\prime}
(see
[
KS22
, Lemma
1.102~(i)
]
). Then
d_{Z}(z,y_{3})=d_{Z}(y,y_{3})-d(y,z)\leq d_{Z}(y,y_{2})+\epsilon^{\prime}+D-d(y,z)\leq d_{Z}(y_{2},z)+\epsilon^{\prime}+D\leq K+2\delta_{0}^{\prime}+\epsilon^{\prime}+D
. So
d_{Z}(y_{2},y_{3})\leq d_{Z}(y_{2},z)+d_{Z}(z,y_{3})\leq 2(K+2\delta_{0}^{\prime})+\epsilon^{\prime}+D=D_{1}
(say).

Hence combining all inequalities, we have

\displaystyle d_{Z}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq d_{Z}(x^{\prime},x_{1})+d_{Z}(x_{1},y_{2})+d_{Z}(y_{2},y_{3})+d_{Z}(y_{3},y_{1})+d_{Z}(y_{1},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},K)+2C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},K)+D_{1}+D=:R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj}}.

Therefore, we are through.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Given
k\geq 0
there is
R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj1}}=R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj1}}(k)
such that the following holds.

Suppose we have assumptions of Lemma
3.3
. Let
A_{v}
be a
k
-quasiconvex subset of
Z_{v}
in
Z_{v}
-metric. Let
x\in Z_{v}
and
y\in Z_{w}
such that
d_{Z}(x,y)=1
, and
x^{\prime}
be a nearest point projection of
x
on
A_{v}
in
Z_{v}
-metric and
y^{\prime}
be that of
y
on
A_{v}
in
Z
-metric. Then
d_{Z}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj1}}
.

Proof.

From the first paragraph of the proof of Lemma
3.4
,
A_{v}
is
K
-quasiconvex in
Z
where
K=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},k)
. If
x_{1}
is a nearest point projection of
x
on
A_{v}
in
Z
-metric, then by Lemma
2.20
(1),
d_{Z}(x_{1},y^{\prime})\leq 2C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},K)
. Again by Lemma
2.23
,
d_{Z}(x^{\prime},x_{1})\leq D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},K)
. Therefore, we can take
R_{\ref{need-in-mitra's-proj1}}:=D_{\ref{for-retraction-flow-space(1)}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{0},K)+C_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta^{\prime}_{0},K)
.
∎

4.
A combination theorem for trees of metric spaces revisited

Suppose
\pi:X\rightarrow T
is a tree of metric spaces (see Definition
3.1
). In this section, we prove the hyperbolicity of
X
within an axiomatic framework (Theorem
1.6
). As a consequence, we get a proof of Theorem
1.4
in Section
10
. Now we will explain the hypotheses. Unless otherwise specified, by
u\in S
(or
v\in S
or
w\in S
) where
S
is a subtree of
T
, we always mean
u
(or
v
or
w
) to be a vertex of
S
. We use the notation
X_{S}:=\pi^{-1}(S)
.

For each vertex
u\in T
there is a subspace, say,
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
containing
X_{u}
and satisfying the following properties
(\mathcal{P}0)-(\mathcal{P}4)
.

(\mathcal{P}0)
Suppose
u,v\in T
and
e
is the edge on
[u,v]
incident on
v
. Let
T^{\prime}
be the maximal subtree of
T
containing
v
but not containing
e
. Then
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{T^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\cap X_{T^{\prime}}
.

(\mathcal{P}1)
Let
L^{\prime}\geq 0
. For each
u\in T
, there is a
L^{\prime}
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction
\rho_{u}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
. We also have an extra property of
\rho_{u}
as follows. Let
T_{u}=\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))
and
e
be an edge in
T
intersecting
T_{u}
at a vertex. Suppose
v
is the vertex adjacent to
e
not in
T_{u}
and
S
is the maximal subtree of
T
containing
v
but not containing
e
. Then diam
\{\rho_{u}(X_{S})\}\leq C
for some uniform constant
C\geq 0
.

(\mathcal{P}2)
There is a threshold constant
L_{0}\geq 0
such that for
L\geq L_{0}
,
N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))
is path connected with the induced path metric from
X
and the inclusion
N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))\hookrightarrow X
is
\eta(L)
-proper embedding.

For
u,v\in T
, we say
[u,v]\subseteq T
is a
special interval
if either
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset
or
X_{u}\cap\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\neq\emptyset
. If
[u,v]
is a special interval then for
L\geq L_{0}
,

(\mathcal{P}3)
the inclusion
N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))\cup N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))\hookrightarrow X
is
\eta^{\prime}(L)
-proper embedding, and

(\mathcal{P}4)

N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))\cup N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))
is
\delta(L)
-hyperbolic metric space.

Definition 4.1
.

For a subtree
S
of
T
, we define
\mathcal{M}(X_{S}):=\cup_{w\in S}\mathcal{M}(X_{w})
. Also for finitely many vertices
u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{n}
, we define
\mathcal{M}(X_{\{u_{1},u_{2},\cdots,u_{n}\}}):=\mathcal{M}(X_{u_{1}})\cup\mathcal{M}(X_{u_{2}})\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{M}(X_{u_{n}})
.

Notation
: For a given subtree
S\subseteq T
, we denote
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S})
to mean
N_{L}(\mathcal{M}(X_{S}))
.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
is divided into two parts as follows.

(1)
Hyperbolictiy of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
where
I
is a special interval in
T
.

(2)
Hyperbolicity of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
where
I
is any interval in
T
.

Finally, using
(2)
, we conclude the proof. Before going into the proof of
(1)
, let us first prove some lemmata which are required in
(1)
and
(2)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
S
be a subtree of
T
. There is a uniform constant
L_{\ref{retraction-gen-abs-flow}}
for which we have a
L_{\ref{retraction-gen-abs-flow}}
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction
\rho_{S}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(X_{S})
.

Proof.

Let us first define
\rho_{S}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(X_{S})
. Let
x\in X
and
u
be the nearest point projection of
\pi(x)
onto
S
. Then
\rho_{S}(x)
is defined to be
\rho_{u}(x)
. Note that if
x\in\mathcal{M}(X_{S})
, then by
(\mathcal{P}0)
,
\rho_{S}(x)=x
.

Let
X_{vsp}=\cup_{u\in T}X_{u}
and
x,y\in X_{vsp}
such that
d_{X}(x,y)\leq 1
. Then by Lemma
2.1
, we need to show a uniform bound on
d_{X}(\rho_{S}(x),\rho_{S}(y))
. Let
u,v
be the nearest point projections of
\pi(x),\pi(y)
on
S
respectively. If
u=v
, then by definition of
\rho_{S}
,
\rho_{S}(x)=\rho_{u}(x)
and
\rho_{S}(y)=\rho_{u}(y)
. So by
(\mathcal{P}1)
,
d_{X}(\rho_{S}(x),\rho_{S}(y))=d_{X}(\rho_{u}(x),\rho_{u}(y))\leq 2L^{\prime}
. Now let
u\neq v
. Since
d_{T}(\pi(x),\pi(y))\leq 1
, we have
x,y\in X_{S}
. So by definition of
\rho_{S}
,
d_{X}(\rho_{S}(x),\rho_{S}(y))\leq 1
. Therefore, we can take
L_{\ref{retraction-gen-abs-flow}}:=C_{\ref{imp-coarse-retraction}}(\textrm{max}\{2L^{\prime},1\})
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
S
be a subtree of
T
. Then for all
L\geq L_{0}
, there is a proper function
\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}=\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L):\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that the inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S})\rightarrow X
is
\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}
-proper embedding.

Proof.

We denote the metric on
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S})
by
d^{\prime}
. Suppose
x_{1},y_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S})
such that
d_{X}(x_{1},y_{1})\leq r
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then there are
x,y\in\mathcal{M}(X_{S})
such that
d^{\prime}(x_{1},x)\leq L
,
d^{\prime}(y_{1},y)\leq L
, and so
d_{X}(x,y)\leq r+2L
. Let
\pi(x)=u
and
\pi(y)=v
, and so
d_{T}(u,v)\leq r
. Let
u^{\prime}
be the nearest point projection of
u
on
S
and
v^{\prime}
be that of
v
on
S
. We consider the following two cases depending on whether
u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
or
u^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime}
.

Case

1
: Suppose
u^{\prime}\neq v^{\prime}
. Let
x^{\prime}\in X_{u^{\prime}}\cap[x,y]
and
y^{\prime}\in X_{v^{\prime}}\cap[x,y]
. Then
d_{X}(x,x^{\prime})\leq r+2L
,
d_{X}(y,y^{\prime})\leq r+2L
, and also
d_{X}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq r+2L
. Now
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u^{\prime}})
implies
d^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})\leq\eta(r+2L)
(by
(\mathcal{P}2)
). Similarly,
d^{\prime}(y,y^{\prime})\leq\eta(r+2L)
. Again
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in X_{S}
implies
d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq d_{X_{S}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\leq\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}(r+2L)
(see Lemma
3.2
). Hence by triangle inequality,
d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq 2\eta(r+2L)+\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}(r+2L)
.

Case

2
: Suppose
u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
. Then by
(\mathcal{P}0)
,
x,y\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u^{\prime}})
. Hence
d^{\prime}(x,y)\leq\eta(r+2L)
.

Therefore, by triangle inequality, in both the cases,
d^{\prime}(x_{1},y_{1})\leq 2L+2\eta(r+2L)+\eta_{\ref{pro-emb}}(r+2L)=:\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L)(r)
.
∎

Hence by Lemma
2.2
, we have the following.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
S
be a subtree of
T
. Then for all
L\geq L_{0}
, there is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}=L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
such that the inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S})\rightarrow X
is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}
-qi embedding.

Proposition 4.5

(Horizontal Subdivision)
.

Let
J=[u,v]\subseteq T
be an interval and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we can subdivide
J
into subintervals
J=J_{0}\cup J_{1}\cup...\cup J_{n-1}
such that
J_{i}=[w_{i},w_{i+1}],~w_{0}=u,~w_{n}=v
and each
J_{i}
is further subdivided into subintervals,
J_{i}=[w_{i},w_{i,1}]\cup[w_{i,1},w_{i,2}]\cup[w_{i,2},w_{i,3}]\cup[w_{i,3},w_{i+1}]
such that the following hold.

(1)

\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}}))\cap J_{i}=[w_{i},w_{i,1}]
,
\forall~0\leq i\leq n-1
.

(2)

For all
i
except possibly
i=n-1
,
d_{T}(w_{i,1},w_{i,2})\leq 2n_{0}
. Also,
[w_{i},w_{i,1}],[w_{i,2},w_{i,3}]
and
[w_{i,3},w_{i+1}]
are special intervals. Moreover,
d_{T}(w_{i,3},w_{i+1})=1
.

(3)

d_{T}(\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}})),\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}})))>2n_{0}
,
\forall~1\leq i\leq n-2
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction. Suppose we have constructed
J_{i-1}
and we want to construct
J_{i}
.

Case 1
: Suppose
\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}})\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset
. Then we stop the process and set
n-1=i,~J_{n-1}=[w_{n-1},v]
and
w_{{n-1},s}=v=w_{n}
for
s=1,2,3
.

Case 2
: Suppose
\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}})\cap X_{v}=\emptyset
. Consider the vertex
w_{i,1}\in(w_{i},v]
in
T
, which is the farthest from
w_{i}
such that
\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}}))\cap[w_{i},v]=[w_{i},w_{i,1}]
. Now we consider the following two subcases.

Subcase (2A)
: Suppose
d_{T}(w_{i,1},\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{v})))\leq 2n_{0}
. Then we consider
w_{i,2}\in[w_{i,1},v]
such that
X_{w_{i,2}}\cap\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\neq\emptyset
and
d_{T}(w_{i,1},w_{i,2})\leq 2n_{0}
. Then we stop the process and set
n-1=i
and
w_{{n-1},3}=v=w_{n}
.

Subcase (2B)
: Suppose
d_{T}(w_{i,1},\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{v})))>2n_{0}
. We take
w_{i+1}\in[w_{i,1},v]
is the farthest from
v
such that
d_{T}(w_{i,1},\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}})))>2n_{0}
. Let
w_{i,3}\in[w_{i,1},w_{i+1}]
such that
d_{T}(w_{i,3},w_{i+1})=1
. Then by our choices,
d_{T}(w_{i,1},\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i,3}})))\leq 2n_{0}
. Now we fix
w_{i,2}\in[w_{i,1},w_{i,3}]
such that
d_{T}(w_{i,1},w_{i,2})\leq 2n_{0}
and
X_{w_{i,2}}\cap\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i,3}})\neq\emptyset
. We also note that
d_{T}(\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}})),\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}})))>2n_{0}
, otherwise,

d_{T}(w_{i,1},\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}})))\leq 2n_{0}.

Therefore, we get
J_{i}=[w_{i},w_{i,1}]\cup[w_{i,1},w_{i,2}]\cup[w_{i,2},w_{i,3}]\cup[w_{i,3},w_{i+1}]
with the required properties.

The induction stops at
(n-1)^{\textrm{th}}
step if
w_{n}=v
. Therefore, we are through.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Suppose
S_{i},i=1,2
are two subtrees in
T
such that
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\{u\}
. Then
\forall~L\geq 0,~\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{1}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{2}})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{1}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{2}})
. For the reverse inclusion, let
x\in\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{1}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{S_{2}})
. Then there exists
x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{S_{i}})
such that
d_{X}(x,x_{i})\leq L
for
i=1,2
. Let
\pi(x_{i})=t_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Now by
(\mathcal{P}0)
, if
d_{T}(t_{2},S_{1})\leq d_{T}(t_{2},S_{2})
then
x_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
or if
d_{T}(t_{1},S_{2})\leq d_{T}(t_{1},S_{1})
then
x_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
. In either case,
x\in\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
. Now suppose
d_{T}(t_{2},S_{1})>d_{T}(t_{2},S_{2})
and
d_{T}(t_{1},S_{2})>d_{T}(t_{1},S_{1})
. Then since
T
is a tree, at least one of the geodesics
[x,x_{1}]_{X}
or
[x,x_{2}]_{X}
has to pass through
X_{u}
. Hence
d_{X}(x,X_{u})\leq L
and so
x\in\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
. Therefore, we are done.
∎

The proof of the upcoming lemma follows from a similar line of reasoning as Lemma
4.6
. So we omit the proof.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose
u,v
and
w
lie on an interval in
T
such that
d_{T}(u,v)\leq d_{T}(u,w)
. Then
\forall~L\geq 0,~\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v\}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{v,w\}})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v})
.

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
L\geq L_{0}
there are constants
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}=\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
and
K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}=K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
satisfying the following. Suppose
u,v
and
w
lie on an interval in
T
such that
d_{T}(u,v)\leq d_{T}(u,w)
and
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset
,
\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\cap X_{w}\neq\emptyset
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
is
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric. Further, the union of any two intersecting spaces among
\{\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w})\}
is
K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}
-quasiconvex in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
.

Proof.

For the first part, we will apply Proposition
2.11
for
n=2
(see Remark
2.12
). Since
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\cap X_{w}\neq\emptyset
, by
(\mathcal{P}4)
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v\}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{v,w\}})
are
\delta(L)
-hyperbolic. Now by Lemma
4.7
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v\}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{v,w\}})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v})
. Again, by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v})
is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded in
X
and so is in both
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v\}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{v,w\}})
with respect to their corresponding path metric.
Thus by Remark
2.12
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v\}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{v,w\}})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
is
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}
-hyperbolic, where
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}:=\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps-2}}(\delta(L),L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L))
.

For the second part, we prove that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,w\}})
is a quasiconvex in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
provided
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w})\neq\emptyset
, and the proof is similar for other intersections. By Lemma
4.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w})
are
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded in
X
and so are in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
. Then by Lemma
2.21

(1)
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w})
are
K_{1}
–quasiconvex in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
, where
K_{1}=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L),L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L),0)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w})
is
K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}
-quasiconvex subset of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
, where
K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}:=K_{1}+\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}
.
∎

Hyperbolicity of
\boldmath{\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})}
where
\boldmath{I}
is a special interval
:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
I
be a special interval in
T
. Then for all
L\geq L_{0}
there is
\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}(L)
such that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is a
\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
.

Proof.

We will apply Proposition
2.10
. Let
I=[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{M}(X_{u^{\prime}})\cap X_{v^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
.

Choices:
For a given
x\in\mathcal{M}(X_{I})
, we fix once and for all
u_{x}\in I
corresponding to
x
such that
x\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u_{x}})
. For a pair
(x,y)
of distinct points
\mathcal{M}(X_{I})
, without loss of generality, we assume that
d_{T}(u^{\prime},u_{x})\leq d_{T}(u^{\prime},u_{y})
. Since
\mathcal{M}(X_{u^{\prime}})\cap X_{v^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, so by
(\mathcal{P}0)
,
\mathcal{M}(X_{u_{x}})\cap X_{u_{y}}\neq\emptyset
. We take
c(x,y)
a fixed geodesic path in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u_{x},u_{y}\}})
. These paths serve as family of paths for Proposition
2.10
.

Note that
\mathcal{M}(X_{I})
is
L
-dense subset in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
. Let
x,y,z\in\mathcal{M}(X_{I})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
x\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u}),~y\in\mathcal{M}(X_{v}),~z\in\mathcal{M}(X_{w})
for
u,v,w\in[u^{\prime},v^{\prime}]
and
d_{T}(u^{\prime},u)\leq d_{T}(u^{\prime},v)\leq d_{T}(u^{\prime},w)
. So by
(\mathcal{P}0)
,
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset,~\mathcal{M}(X_{v})\cap X_{w}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})\cap X_{w}\neq\emptyset
.

Condition (1)
: Let
s,t\in\{u,v,w\}
and
s\neq t
. By
(\mathcal{P}3)
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}})
is
\eta^{\prime}(L)
-properly embedded in
X
and so is in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
. Hence the family of paths are
\eta^{\prime}(L)
-properly embedded in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
.

Condition (2)
: We want to prove that the triangle formed by paths
c(x,y),~c(y,z)
and
c(x,z)
is uniformly slim.
Then by Lemma
4.8
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
-hyperbolic with the induced path metric and for all distinct
s,t\in\{u,v,w\}
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}})
is
K_{1}
-quasiconvex in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
, where
K_{1}=K_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
. Now we will show that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}})
is uniformly qi embedded in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
where
s\neq t
and
s,t\in\{u,v,w\}
. Let
N^{\prime}_{K_{1}+1}(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}}))\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
denote
(K_{1}+1)
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}})
in metric of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
. Hence by Lemma
2.22
,
N^{\prime}_{(K_{1}+1)}(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}}))
is
L_{1}
-qi embedded in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
for some
L_{1}
depending on
\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
and
K_{1}
. Hence by Lemma
2.3
, the inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{s,t\}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
is
L_{2}
-qi embedding, where
L_{2}=L_{\ref{hd-imp-qi}}(\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L),K_{1}+1)
.

So by the stability of quasi-geodesic (see Lemma
2.5
), there is constant
D=2D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}},L_{2},L_{2})+\delta_{\ref{union-uvw-is-hyperbolic}}(L)
such that the triangle formed by paths
c(x,y)
,
c(y,z)
,
c(x,z)
is
D
-slim in the metric of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{\{u,v,w\}})
and so is in the metric of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
.

Therefore, by Proposition
2.10
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is
\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic, where
\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{combing}}(\eta^{\prime}(L),D,L)
.
∎

As an iterated application of Proposition
4.9
along with Proposition
2.11
for
n=2
(see Remark
2.12
), we obtain the following. The proof is omitted.

Lemma 4.10
.

Given
L\geq L_{0}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
\delta_{\ref{hyp-of-depending-on-length}}=\delta_{\ref{hyp-of-depending-on-length}}(L,l)
satisfying the following. Let
I=[u,v]
for
u,v\in T
such that
d_{T}(u,v)\leq l
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is a
\delta_{\ref{hyp-of-depending-on-length}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
.

Hyperbolicity of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
where
I
is any interval
: Before going into the proof, we first prove the following two lemmata which will be used in the proof.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
J=\cup_{i=1}^{4}J_{i}\subseteq T
is an interval in
T
such that length of
J_{2}\leq l
. Further
J_{1},J_{3},J_{4}
are special intervals, and
J_{i}\cap J_{i+1}
is a single vertex for
1\leq i\leq 3
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
J
is as described above. For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
L\geq L_{0}
there exists
\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}(L,l)
such that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J})
is
\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
.

Proof.

We will apply Proposition
2.11
for
n=2
three times, successively on pairs
(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{2}}))
,
(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}\cup J_{2}}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{3}}))
and
(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup J_{3}}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{4}}))
. Since
J_{1},J_{3},J_{4}
are special interval, by Proposition
4.9
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{i}})
is
\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}(L)
-hyperbolic for
i=1,3,4
; and by Lemma
4.10
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{2}})
is
\delta_{\ref{hyp-of-depending-on-length}}(L,l)
-hyperbolic. Suppose
\delta_{1}=max\{\delta_{\ref{special-int-sp-is-hyp}}(L),\delta_{\ref{hyp-of-depending-on-length}}(L,l)\}
. Let
\{u\}=J_{1}\cap J_{2}
. Then by Lemma
4.6
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}})\cap\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{2}})
. Again by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded in
X
and so is in both
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{2}})
in their respective path metric.
Therefore, by Remark
2.12
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{2}})
is
\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps-2}}(\delta_{1},L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L))
-hyperbolic.

Applying the similar argument as above on the remaining pairs we have mentioned, we conclude that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{1}\cup J_{2}\cup J_{3}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{4}})=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J})
is uniformly hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
. Let the uniform constant be
\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}(L,l)
.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Given
\delta\geq 0,~L\geq L_{0}
and a proper function
g:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, there is a constant
D_{\ref{cobounded-flow-sps}}=D_{\ref{cobounded-flow-sps}}(\delta,L,g)
such that the following holds.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be a
\delta
-hyperbolic subspace of
X
such that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v})\subseteq Y
and
\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))\cap\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))=\emptyset
. Suppose
Y
is
g
-properly embedded in
X
. Then the pair
(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v}))
is
D_{\ref{cobounded-flow-sps}}
-cobounded in
Y
.

Proof.

We first note that by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded in
X
and so is in
Y
. Then by Lemma
2.21

(1)
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u})
is
K_{1}
-quasiconvex in
Y
, where
K_{1}=K_{\ref{quasi-goes-to-quasi}}(\delta_{1},L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L),0)
. Hence
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
is
K_{2}
-quasiconvex in
Y
, where
K_{2}=K_{1}+L
. Similarly,
\mathcal{M}(X_{v})
is
K_{2}
-quasiconvex in
Y
.

Let
p:Y\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
be a nearest point projection map on
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
in the metric of
Y
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{M}(X_{v})
such that
p(x)=x_{1},~p(y)=y_{1}
. By Lemma
2.20

(3)
and the symmetry of the proof, it is enough to show that
d_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})
is uniformly bounded.

Now by
[
MS12
, Lemma
1.31

(2)
]
, the arc-length parametrizations of
[x,x_{1}]_{Y}\cup[x_{1},y_{1}]_{Y}
and
[y,y_{1}]_{Y}\cup[y_{1},x_{1}]_{Y}
are
(3+2K_{2})
-quasi-geodesic in
Y
. If
d_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})\leq L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,3+2K_{2},3+2K_{2})
, then we are done. Suppose
d_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})>L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,3+2K_{2},3+2K_{2})
. So by Lemma
2.9
,
[x,x_{1}]_{Y}\cup[x_{1},y_{1}]_{Y}\cup[y_{1},y]_{Y}
is
\lambda_{1}
-quasi-geodesic in
Y
, where
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,3+2K_{2},3+2K_{2})
. Therefore, by stability of quasi-geodesic (see Lemma
2.5
) and
K_{2}
-quasiconvexity of
\mathcal{M}(X_{v})
in
Y
, there exist
x_{2},y_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{v})
such that
d_{Y}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1})+K_{2}=L_{1}
(say) and
d_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2})\leq L_{1}
. Since
\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}))\cap\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))=\emptyset
, by
(\mathcal{P}1)
, diam
\{\rho_{u}(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))\}\leq C
in
X
. Therefore,
x_{2},y_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{v})
implies
d_{X}(\rho_{u}(x_{2}),\rho_{u}(y_{2}))\leq C
. Again
\rho_{u}
is
L^{\prime}
-coarsely Lipschitz retraction of
X
on
\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
(see
(\mathcal{P}1)
). Since
x_{1},y_{1}\in\mathcal{M}(X_{u})
, so
\rho_{u}(x_{1})=x_{1},~\rho_{u}(y_{1})=y_{1}
. So
d_{X}(x_{1},\rho_{u}(x_{2}))=d_{X}(\rho_{u}(x_{1}),\rho_{u}(x_{2}))\leq L^{\prime}L_{1}+L^{\prime}=L_{2}
(say). Similarly,
d_{X}(y_{1},\rho_{u}(y_{2}))\leq L_{2}
. Therefore,
d_{X}(x_{1},y_{1})\leq d_{X}(x_{1},\rho(x_{2}))+d_{X}(\rho_{u}(x_{2}),\rho_{u}(y_{2}))+d_{X}(\rho_{u}(y_{2}),y_{1})\leq 2L_{2}+C

\Rightarrow d_{Y}(x_{1},y_{1})\leq g(2L_{2}+C)
since
Y
is
g
-properly embedded in
X
.

Hence, diam
\{p(\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))\}\leq L_{3}
in
Y
, where
L_{3}=\textrm{max}\{g(2L_{2}+C),~L_{\ref{lo_vs_gl}}(\delta,3+2K_{2},3+2K_{2})\}
. Therefore, (by the symmetry of the proof) the pair
(\mathcal{M}(X_{u}),\mathcal{M}(X_{v}))
is
L_{3}
-cobounded in
Y
. Then by Lemma
2.20

(3)
, the pair
(\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{u}),\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{v}))
is
D_{\ref{cobounded-flow-sps}}
-cobounded in
Y
, where
D_{\ref{cobounded-flow-sps}}=D_{\ref{proj-on-qc}}(\delta_{1},K_{2},L_{3},L)
.
∎

Now we are ready to proof the main result.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
I
be an interval in
T
. Then for all
L\geq L_{0}
there is
\delta_{\ref{general-int-sp-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{general-int-sp-is-hyp}}(L)
such that
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is
\delta_{\ref{general-int-sp-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
.

Proof.

Let
I=J_{0}\cup J_{1}\cup\cdots\cup J_{n-1}
be a subdivision of the interval
I
coming from horizontal subdivision, Proposition
4.5
, with
n_{0}=[L]+2
, where
[L]
is the greatest integer not greater than
L
. We refer to Proposition
4.5
for the description of
J_{i}=[w_{i},w_{i+1}]
. Then by Lemma
4.11
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{i}})
is
\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}(L,2n_{0})
-hyperbolic metric space for all
i\in\{0,1,\cdots,n-1\}
.

Now we will verify all the conditions of Proposition
2.11
. Let
X_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{J_{i}})
for
0\leq i\leq n-1
and
Y_{i+1}=X_{i}\cap X_{i+1}=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w_{i+1}})
for
0\leq i\leq n-2
(see Lemma
4.6
).

(1)
For
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
X_{i}
is
\delta_{1}
-hyperbolic metric space, where
\delta_{1}=\delta_{\ref{union-of-three-special-int-is-hyp}}(L,2n_{0})
.

(2)
By Lemma
4.4
,
Y_{i+1}=\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w_{i+1}})
is
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded in
X
so is in both
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
for
0\leq i\leq n-2
.

(3)
Note that by
(\mathcal{P}0)
, if
x\in X_{i}\setminus Y_{i+1}
and
y\in X_{i+1}\setminus Y_{i+1}
then
w_{i+1}\in[\pi(x),\pi(y)]\setminus\{\pi(x),\pi(y)\}
. Hence every path in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
joining points
X_{i}
and
X_{i+1}
passes through
Y_{i+1}
.

(4)
Suppose
i\in\{1,2,\cdots,n-2\}
. Note that
\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}}))\cap\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}}))=\emptyset
(by Proposition
4.5

(3)
). Again
X_{i}
is
\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L)
-properly embedded in
X
and so is in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{i})
. Also,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w_{i}})\cup\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{w_{i+1}})\subseteq X_{i}
. Then by Lemma
4.12
, there is
D
depending on
\delta_{1}
,
L
and
\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L)
such that the pair
(Y_{i},Y_{i+1})
is
D
-cobounded in
X_{i}
.

(5)
Let
1\leq i\leq n-2
and
d_{X_{i}}(Y_{i},Y_{i+1})\leq 1
. Then
d_{X}(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}}),\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}}))\leq 2L+1
, and so
d_{T}(\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i}})),\pi(\mathcal{M}(X_{w_{i+1}})))\leq 2L+1\leq 2n_{0}
(by our choice of
n_{0}
). This contradicts to
(3)
of Proposition
4.5
.

Hence by Proposition
2.11
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{I})
is
\delta_{\ref{general-int-sp-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic, where
\delta_{\ref{general-int-sp-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{combi-hyp-sps}}(\delta_{1},L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L),D)
.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
4.13
along with Proposition
2.11
, we obtain following. We omit the proof.

Lemma 4.14
.

Given
L\geq L_{0}
there is
\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}=\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}(L)
satisfying the following. Let
u,v,w\in T
and
T_{uvw}
be the tripod in
T
with vertices
u,v,w
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
is
\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}
-hyperbolic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
: We fix
L=L_{0}
. We show that
X
satisfies all the conditions of Proposition
2.10
. Let
X_{vsp}=\cup_{u\in T}X_{u}
. Note that
X_{vsp}
is a
1
-dense subspace of
X
. So given any two points
x,y\in X_{vsp}
, we define path joining them as follows:

Let
x\in X_{u}
and
y\in X_{v}
for some
u,v\in T
. Note that
X_{[u,v]}=\pi^{-1}([u,v])
. We fix once and for all, a geodesic path
c(x,y)
in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{[u,v]})
joining
x
and
y
. These paths serve as family of paths for Proposition
2.10
.

Let
x,y,z\in V(X)
such that
\pi(x)=u,~\pi(y)=v
and
\pi(z)=w
.

Condition (1)
: For all distinct
s,t\in\{u,v,w\}
, by Proposition
4.3
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{[s,t]})
is
\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L)
-properly embedded in
X
and so are the paths.

Condition (2)
: Let
T_{uvw}
be the tripod in
T
with vertices
\{u,v,w\}
. By Lemma
4.14
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
is
\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}(L)
-hyperbolic metric space. Again by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{[u,v]}),~\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{[v,w]})
and
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{[u,w]})
are
L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L)
-qi embedded subspaces of
X
and so are of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
. Then the hyperbolicity of
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
and the stability of quasi-geodesic in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
(see Lemma
2.5
) imply that the triangle formed by paths
c(x,y),~c(y,z)
and
c(x,z)
is
D
-slim in
\mathcal{M}_{L}(X_{T_{uvw}})
and so is in
X
, where

D=2D_{\ref{ml}}(\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}(L),L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L),L_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-qi-emb}}(L))+\delta_{\ref{tripod-sp-is-hyp}}(L).

Hence by Proposition
2.10
,
X
is
\delta_{\ref{combing}}(\eta_{\ref{gen-abs-flow-pro-emb}}(L),D,1)
-hyperbolic. This completes the proof.∎

5.
Trees of metric bundles and their properties

The notion of metric bundles (see Definition
5.1
) was introduced by Mj and Sardar (
[
MS12
]
). Subsuming both metric bundles and trees of metric spaces, we define trees of metric bundles (Definition
5.2
).

Definition 5.1
.

(
[
MS12
, Definition
1.2
]
)

Suppose
(X,d)
and
(B,d_{B})
are geodesic metric spaces; let
c_{0}\geq 1
and
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a proper map. We say
X
is a
(\phi,c_{0})
-
metric bundle
over
B
if there is a
1
-Lipschitz and surjective map
p:X\rightarrow B
such that the following holds.

(1)

Let
z\in B
. Then
F_{z}:=p^{-1}(z)
, called fiber, is a geodesic metric space with the induced path metric from
X
and the inclusion
F_{z}\hookrightarrow X
is
\phi
-proper embedding.

(2)

Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in B
such that
d_{B}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq 1
and
\alpha
be a geodesic joining
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. Then for all
z\in\alpha
and
x\in F_{z}
, there are paths in
p^{-1}(\alpha)
of length at most
c_{0}
joining
x
to points in
F_{z_{1}}
and
F_{z_{2}}
.

Definition 5.2

(
Trees of metric bundles).

Let
(X,d)
be a geodesic metric space. Suppose
\pi_{B}:(B,d_{B})\rightarrow T
is a tree of spaces over a tree
T
such that edge spaces are points. Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a proper map and
c_{0}\geq 1
. A tree of metric bundles is a
1
-Lipschitz surjective map
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow B
such that the following hold (see Figure
1
).

(1)

For all
u\in V(T)
let
B_{u}:=\pi_{B}^{-1}(u)
and
X_{u}:=\pi_{X}^{-1}(B_{u})
. Then
X_{u}
is geodesic metric space with the induced path metric and the restriction of
\pi_{X}
to
X_{u}
gives a
(\phi,c_{0})
-metric bundle
X_{u}\rightarrow B_{u}
(see Definition
5.1
).

(2)

Let
e=[v,w]
be an edge in
T
, and
\mathfrak{e}=[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}]
be the lift of
e
joining
\mathfrak{v}\in B_{v}
and
\mathfrak{w}\in B_{w}
. Then
\pi_{X}
restricted to
\pi_{X}^{-1}(\mathfrak{e})
is a tree metric spaces over
\mathfrak{e}
with parameter
\phi
(see Definition
3.1
)

(3)

For
u\in V(T)
and
a\in B_{u}
, we denote the fiber corresponding to
a
by
F_{a,u}(:=\pi_{X}^{-1}(a))
. Then the inclusion
F_{a,u}\hookrightarrow X
is
\phi
-proper embedding.

Abusing terminology, we say
(X,B,T)
is a tree of metric bundles keeping the structural maps
\pi_{X}
,
\pi_{B}
and parameters
\phi
,
c_{0}
and other things implicit. We denote the composition of
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow B
and
\pi_{B}:B\rightarrow T
by
\pi:X\rightarrow T
.

Figure 1
.
Trees of Metric Bundles

Disclaimer
: The term ‘trees of metric bundles’ may be misleading for the map
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow B
since
B
is not a tree in general; but it is not misleading for
\pi=\pi_{B}\circ\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow T
. To maintain consistency with existing literature, we will adhere to our chosen nomenclature.

We will see some properties of a tree of metric bundles
(X,B,T)
that are used in the main proof. In our statements, we make the structural parameters
\phi,c_{0}
implicit.

For
u\in V(T)
, since fibers are
\phi
-properly embedded in
X
, we can show (along the same line of arguments given in the proof of
[
KS22
, Proposition
2.17
]
; see Lemma
3.2
) that
X_{u}:=\pi^{-1}(u)
is uniformly properly embedded in
X
. Then considering
(X,B,T)
as a tree of metric spaces
\pi:=\pi_{B}\circ\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow T
, we get the following as corollary of Lemma
3.2
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose
(X,B,T)
is a tree of metric bundles. Let
S
be a subtree of
T
and
X_{S}:=\pi^{-1}(S)
. We consider
X_{S}
with the path metric induced from
X
. Then there exists a proper function
\eta_{\ref{subsp-is-proper-emb}}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
depending only on the structural parameters of
(X,B,T)
such that the inclusion
i:X_{S}\hookrightarrow X
is
\eta_{\ref{subsp-is-proper-emb}}
-proper embedding.

Definition 5.4

(Quasi-isometric (qi) section)
.

Let
K\geq 1
. Suppose
(X,B,T)
is a tree of metric bundles. Let
B_{1}
be an isometrically embedded subspace in
B
and
X_{1}\subseteq X
. We say
X_{1}
is
K
-qi section in
X
over
B_{1}
if there is a
K
-qi embedding
s:B_{1}\rightarrow X
such that
\pi_{X}\circ s=id
on
B_{1}
and
X_{1}=Im(s)
. Further, we say that it is compatible if the following hold.

(1)

For all
w\in\pi_{B}(B_{1})
,
X_{1}\cap X_{w}
is
K
-qi section over
B_{1}\cap B_{w}
in the path metric of
X_{w}
and
X_{1}\cap X=Im(s|_{B_{1}\cap B_{w}})
.

(2)

Suppose
[v,w]\subseteq\pi_{B}(B_{1})
is an edge, and
[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}]
is the edge joining
\mathfrak{v}\in B_{v}
and
\mathfrak{w}\in B_{w}
. Then
s(\mathfrak{v})
and
s(\mathfrak{w})
are
K
-apart in the path metric on
\pi_{X}^{-1}([\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}])
induced from
X
.

Definition 5.5
.

If
B_{1}
, in Definition
5.4
, is a geodesic segment, say,
\alpha:[0,r]\subseteq\mathbb{R}\rightarrow B
, then we call the section a
K
-
qi lift
of the geodesic
\alpha
. According to our definition, a
K
-qi lift of a geodesic
\alpha:[0,r]\subseteq\mathbb{R}\rightarrow B
is
Im(\tilde{\alpha})
where
\tilde{\alpha}:Im(\alpha)\subseteq B\rightarrow X
is a
K
-qi embedding. We will simultaneously use
Im(\alpha)
and
[0,r]
as the domain of
\tilde{\alpha}
.

Existence of uniform qi section in metric bundle was one of the difficult jobs in
[
MS12
]
